Households deleverage – governments do not
The global household-debt ratio fell to 60.9% of GDP
in 2025, almost 10pps below its 2005 level and nearly
13pps below its 2009 peak (Figure 21). This does not
mean that households stopped borrowing: liabilities
continued to increase, but more slowly than nominal
economic output. Nevertheless, the contrast with the
public sector is striking. While government debt has
continued to rise, households have spent much of the

past decade strengthening their balance sheets.
But deleveraging was far from universal. Since 2005, the
debt ratio has fallen by 20.4pps in North America and
by 6.3pps in Western Europe, while Japan’s ratio has
remained broadly stable. Australia and New Zealand
stand apart from this trend: their combined ratio rose by
12.2pps to 113.8%, although it remains below its 2020
peak. The decline in the global ratio therefore conceals
a significant shift in the geography of household debt,

from the traditional advanced economies towards
emerging markets, albeit with important exceptions.
China recorded by far the largest increase. Its
household-debt ratio rose by 42.1pps to 59.4% of GDP
between 2005 and 2025, reflecting the extraordinary
credit and property boom of the intervening years.
However, the ratio has barely changed since 2020,
underlining how abruptly that boom has ended. China’s
household-debt ratio is now only around 10pps below

that of the US, but the two countries have followed
opposite paths: American households have deleveraged
substantially, while China has moved from rapid debt
accumulation to balance-sheet caution.
Elsewhere in the emerging world, the increase was less
dramatic. Since 2005, the household-debt ratio has
risen by 10.7pps in Latin America to 27.8% and by 6.1pps
in Eastern Europe to 21.8%. Both remain moderate by
international standards. Asia excluding Japan and China

also appears relatively stable, with its aggregate ratio
rising by only 2.2pps to 55.1%. But this regional average
is deceptive. It partly reflects the growing weight
of poorer, less-indebted economies and substantial
deleveraging in Singapore, where the ratio fell by
26.4pps to 51.6%.
At the country level, the fault lines cut across the
advanced and emerging-market divide. Switzerland and
Australia recorded debt ratios of 125.1% and 119.4% of
GDP , respectively, while Canada stood at 103.3% and

the Netherlands at 97.2%. Several Asian economies
also carry substantial household-debt burdens: Taiwan
and South Korea recorded ratios of around 92%, while
Thailand and Malaysia stood at approximately 87%
and 85%, respectively. These levels are comparable
with those recorded in several Western economies
before the global financial crisis. In 2006, household
debt stood at 98.4% of GDP in the US, 84.2% in Spain
and 95.0% in Ireland. High leverage does not in itself

signal an imminent crisis, but it makes households and
consumption more vulnerable to income, interest-rate
and housing-market shocks. The decisive dividing line is
therefore not between advanced and emerging markets,
but between housing and financial systems that rely
heavily on household leverage and those that do not.

Strong asset growth and subdued borrowing pushed

global net financial assets (financial assets minus
liabilities) up by +10.1% in 2025, broadly matching
the previous year’s +10.2%. They reached a record
EUR211.5trn, almost twice their 2015 level and more
than tripling their value in 2005.
China recorded the strongest increase in net financial
wealth at +15.3%, reflecting the combination of strong
asset growth and the near-standstill in household debt.
Latin America followed at +11.3%, while Eastern Europe

and Asia excluding China and Japan both recorded
growth of +11.0%. North America and Australia and
New Zealand also achieved double-digit increases
of +10.4% each. Japan recorded a strong +7.4%, while
Western Europe again brought up the rear at +5.2%.
Growth exceeded its 20-year average in almost every
region (Figure 22). Latin America was the only exception,
although its +11.3% increase was broadly in line with its
long-term rate.
The figures reveal a less visible source of wealth

creation. Weak credit growth may constrain housing
activity and household investment, but it also
strengthens private balance sheets when financial
assets continue to expand. In 2025, this deleveraging
dividend lifted global net financial wealth faster than
gross financial wealth. It also reinforced North America’s
dominant position: the region held 51.3% of global net
financial assets, compared with 48.1% of gross financial
assets. Low leverage relative to its enormous asset base

gives North America an additional wealth advantage.
Adjusted for population growth and inflation, China
remains the undisputed long-term leader (Figure 23).
Real net financial assets per capita have increased
more than sevenfold over the past two decades. No
other region comes close. Even the other emerging-
market regions recorded little more than a doubling.
Among advanced markets, Western Europe continued
to lag behind Japan, highlighting how weak asset

growth, rather than debt accumulation, has constrained
European wealth creation.
Comparing gross and net financial assets adds another
dimension. In China, net financial assets grew around
0.7pps more slowly per year than gross financial
assets over the past two decades, reflecting the rapid
accumulation of household debt. A smaller gap is
also visible in Eastern Europe. However, this is not a
universal emerging-market pattern: in Latin America
and the rest of Asia, gross and net wealth developed at

broadly similar rates. In the advanced economies, the
relationship is reversed. As household debt generally
grew more slowly than assets, net financial wealth
outpaced gross wealth. The difference was particularly
pronounced in North America, where net financial assets
grew around 0.7pps faster per year.

Examining the 25 richest countries by net financial assets
per capita at the end of 2025 reveals how significantly
debt can alter the ranking (Figure 24). Most notably,
the US ranks ahead of Switzerland to claim first place.
Although Switzerland remains ahead in gross financial
assets, its household liabilities of around EUR130,080
per capita are more than twice the US figure of
EUR52,730.
Taiwan and Sweden exchange fifth and sixth places.

Australia falls from eighth place in the gross ranking to
eleventh in the net ranking, while Ireland slips from 14th
to 15th, the UK from 15th to 18th and Norway from 12th
to 22nd. By contrast, Germany rises from 13th to 12th,
Italy from 16th to 13th, Japan from 18th to 14th and
Spain from 22nd to 21st. The Netherlands remains ninth
in both rankings. The comparison makes clear that gross
wealth tells only half the story: what ultimately matters
is how much remains after debt.

After a temporary acceleration to +4.7% in 2024,
growth in global real estate assets12 slowed again to
+3.5% in 2025. Apart from 2023, growth was weaker
only in the aftermath of the global financial crisis in
2012. Persistently high construction costs and elevated
mortgage rates continued to weigh on affordability,
transactions and new development. Overall, the value of
real estate assets in the countries covered by the report

reached EUR155.0trn (Figure 25)
Regional developments varied widely (Figure 26).
Eastern Europe recorded the strongest increase at
+10.6%, followed by Australia and New Zealand at
+8.5%. Both comfortably exceeded their long-term
growth rates despite the pressure from higher financing
costs. Japan also stood out: real-estate assets increased
by +5.0%, compared with a long-term average of
just +0.7%. Together with the recent acceleration in
household borrowing, this provides further evidence that

Japan may finally be leaving its long deflationary period
behind.
Western Europe recorded growth of +4.1%, slightly
above its long-term average of +3.5%. North America
meanwhile was the clear exception: growth slowed
sharply from +4.9% in 2024 to just +1.8% in 2025,
less than half its long-term rate of +3.9%. The global
slowdown was therefore driven primarily by the largest
housing market.

As with financial assets, a clearer picture emerges
when real-estate wealth is measured per capita and
adjusted for inflation (Figure 27). The long shadow of
Japan’s property crash remains visible: over the past two
decades, real-estate wealth per capita was essentially
flat in real terms, despite the recent recovery.
Real gains were positive in Australia and New Zealand,
North America and Western Europe, but generally
modest. Compared with financial assets, the gap was

particularly pronounced in North America, where real
financial assets per capita grew around 2pps faster
per year, and in Japan, where the gap was around
1.3pps. In Australia and New Zealand and Western
Europe, by contrast, the difference was only around
0.2pps annually. Property may provide stability, housing
services and diversification, but capital appreciation
alone has generally been a weaker engine of wealth
creation than participation in financial markets.

The country ranking of real estate assets nevertheless
shows how strongly housing can reshape the overall
picture. Switzerland had the highest real-estate assets
per capita at EUR350,450, followed by Australia
at EUR279,310 with the US taking the 7th spot at
EUR174,030. But what does the ranking look like when
net financial assets and real estate assets are presented
together? Figure 28 provides the answer. Switzerland
overtakes the US and claims first place, with combined

wealth of around EUR626,430 per capita. Australia
meanwhile is the biggest beneficiary, climbing seven
places from tenth in the adjusted net-financial-assets
ranking to third once property is included. Sweden
and Japan each fall six places, while Italy drops four.
Germany moves up one position to tenth. These shifts
expose two very different models of household wealth:
some countries build wealth predominantly through
financial markets, while others hold a much larger share

in bricks and mortar.

International: Catch-up loses momentum
For many years, global wealth development was
characterized by convergence, as emerging markets
gradually closed the gap with richer economies. That
process has not ended, but it has slowed dramatically.
The ratio of average net financial assets per capita in
advanced economies to those in emerging markets
fell from 62 in 2005 to 21 in 2015. This means that, on
average, the net financial assets of richer countries were

“only” 21 times higher than those of poorer countries in
2015. Over the following decade, however, it declined by
less than four points to 17 in 2025 (Figure 29).
Convergence reversed from 2017. After narrowing
rapidly for more than a decade, the wealth gap widened
again between 2017 and 2021. Convergence resumed
in 2022, but at a much slower pace: the ratio has fallen
from 18.5 to 17.2 in the past three years. The global
wealth gap is still closing, but has slowed to a crawl.

Declaring the end of globalization would be premature,
but its character has clearly changed. Geopolitical
tensions, weaker cross-border investment and the
reorganization of supply chains are eroding some of
the advantages that emerging markets derived from
ever-deeper global integration. At the same time, the
green transition and AI could create new opportunities
by increasing demand for critical resources, energy
infrastructure, data services and digitally delivered

work. Whether emerging economies can seize these
opportunities will depend on their access to technology,
capital and skills. For now, these transformations are
unfolding in a more fragmented world, creating a less
favorable environment for poorer countries to catch up.
Against this backdrop, the global wealth middle class
comprised around 866mn people in 2025.13 Although
its size changed little compared with the previous
year, its long-term expansion remains impressive:

membership has increased by 81% since 2005. More
importantly, its center of gravity has shifted. Emerging
markets accounted for 61.9% of its members in 2025,
up from 43.6% two decades earlier, with China alone
representing slightly more than half of the emerging-
market middle class.
Distribution:
New wealth, old divides

The transformation is even more striking among the
wealthy. The global high-wealth class grew by 37%

to around 584mn people between 2005 and 2025.
Emerging markets accounted for 32.1% of this group,
compared with less than 2% in 2005. This expansion
came entirely from emerging markets, while the number
of high-wealth individuals in advanced economies
declined slightly. China played the dominant role,
accounting for more than three-quarters of the
emerging-market total.
Emerging economies also generated most of the
growth in the global middle class. Taken together,

they accounted for more than 90% of the increase in
the middle and high-wealth classes over the past two
decades. The global wealth landscape has therefore
become far more diverse, even though the gap in
average wealth between advanced and emerging
markets remains wide.
The global low-wealth class grew by around 7% to
4.3bn people, while the population covered by the
analysis increased by almost 17%. Its share of the total
population consequently fell from 81.7% in 2005 to

74.8% in 2025. Emerging markets still accounted for
92.0% of this group, slightly below their 92.9% share two
decades earlier. However, the number of low-wealth
individuals in advanced economies increased by around
21% to 346mn. The global shift towards higher wealth
classes is therefore unmistakable, but it has by no means
benefited everyone.
The emergence of a larger and more geographically
diverse high-wealth class does not change the fact that

financial wealth remains extremely concentrated at the
global level. This becomes clear when the population of
the countries covered by the report is divided into deciles
according to net financial assets.
In 2025, the richest 10%, with average net financial
assets of approximately EUR312,590, owned 85.4% of
global net financial wealth. Their share has declined
from 91.5% in 2005, but remains far above the average
national top-decile share of 60.9% (Figure 30). At the

pace of improvement recorded over the past two
decades, it would take almost another eight decades
for global concentration to approach the level currently
observed within the countries.
13  The classification in wealth classes is based on worldwide average net financial assets per capita, which stood at EUR36,690 in 2025. The global
wealth middle class („middle wealth“, MW) includes all individuals with net financial assets of between 30% and 180% of the global average. This

means that for 2025, the rounded asset thresholds for the global wealth middle class are EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. The „low wealth“ (LW) catego -
ry, on the other hand, includes those individuals with net financial assets that are below a EUR11,000 threshold, while the term „high wealth“ (HW)
applies to those with net financial assets of more than EUR66,000 (for details on how the asset thresholds are set, see Appendix A).

### Page 38

Allianz Research
38
High global wealth concentration is also reflected in the
wide gap between median and average net financial
assets. In 2025, global median net financial wealth
stood at approximately EUR2,490 per capita, while the
average was almost 15 times as high at EUR36,690. The
gap, however, has narrowed substantially over time: in
2005, the average was almost 33 times the median. The
typical person has therefore gained ground relative to

the global average, but the average remains a poor
representation of ordinary wealth.
At first glance, the much faster growth of average net
wealth in most lower deciles appears encouraging
(Figure 31). However, these high growth rates largely
reflect extremely low starting points. Even large
percentage increases translate into modest absolute
gains, while the richest groups continue to accumulate
much larger sums. A more equal global distribution of

wealth therefore remains a distant prospect.

Bottom 50%
The gap between the richest decile and everyone else is

enormous. The second-richest decile owned just 8.7% of
global net financial assets, with an average of around
EUR34,060 per person. The richest tenth therefore held
nine times as much wealth per person as the group
immediately below it.
The bottom half of the population covered by the
report, comprising almost 3bn people, held virtually
nothing. Its combined share of global net financial
assets was approximately zero. The bottom decile had
negative net financial assets of around EUR2,680 per

person, as liabilities exceeded financial assets. Even
in the two deciles around the middle of the global
distribution, average net financial assets amounted to
only approximately EUR3,170 and EUR1,810 per person.
Global wealth has become more geographically diverse,
but it has hardly become broadly distributed.

### Page 39

29 September  2026
39
National: More wealth, same divide
Net financial wealth is distributed less unevenly within
countries than across the world. In 2025, the richest 10%
owned an average of 60.9% of national net financial
assets across the 57 countries examined, using an
unweighted average, compared with 85.4% at the global
level. The gap between average and median wealth
was also considerably smaller: the national average-
to-median ratio was 3.12, again measured as an

unweighted average.
When it comes to inequality, however, the national
picture is particularly disappointing. Despite being a
major political issue for years, there has been virtually
no progress towards more even wealth distribution.
In 2005, the richest 10% held an average of 60.4% of
national wealth, while the average-to-median ratio
stood at 3.11. Two decades later, both measures are
almost unchanged. Wealth has grown substantially, but
its distribution within individual countries has remained

remarkably resistant to change.
This overall stability conceals sharper shifts at the
country level. In 32 of the 57 countries examined, the
share of the richest 10% changed by less than 2pps
between 2005 and 2025 (Figure 32). Where larger
shifts occurred, however, the balance was clearly tilted
towards greater inequality, with deterioration almost
twice as likely as improvement. Wealth concentration
increased in 16 countries and declined in only nine.

Among those recording an improvement, Cambodia
saw the largest decline in the top-decile share, followed
by Portugal and Slovakia. Latvia, Hungary, Belgium,
Ireland, Malaysia and the Netherlands also registered
meaningful decreases. Different starting points were at
work. Portugal, Latvia and Ireland moved down from
relatively high levels of concentration, while Slovakia,
Hungary and the Netherlands improved from already
more balanced positions. In the Netherlands, the richest

10% saw their share fall by 2.4pps to 56.1%.
At the other end of the spectrum, China recorded by far
the largest increase in concentration. The share of the
richest 10% rose by 15.7pps between 2005 and 2025 to
68.0%, well above the average national share of 60.9%.
This accompanied the enormous economic and social
transformation of the past two decades, which created
not only broad-based wealth but also a substantial
private upper class. China illustrates the difficulty of

combining rapid wealth creation with an equally rapid
diffusion of its benefits. After all, it was the unbridled
growth of the „wild“ 2000s, when the private sector
was subject to few restrictions, that primarily caused
inequality to rise. In sharp contrast, in the last five years,
the concentration of wealth has remained unchanged.

The ratio of average to median wealth provides an
important cross-check: it shows whether rising average
wealth is also reflected in the position of the typical

household. Across the 57 countries examined, the
average ratio remained virtually unchanged, rising from
3.11 in 2005 to 3.12 in 2025. This confirms the broader
picture of persistent national wealth inequality.
The strongest improvements occurred in the
Netherlands, where the ratio fell from 4.04 to 2.48, and
Ireland, where it declined from 4.87 to 3.42. In both
countries, the narrowing gap indicates that the middle
of the distribution gained ground relative to average

wealth.
The largest deteriorations were recorded in Russia,
Sweden and China. Russia’s ratio rose from 2.99 to 4.77,
while Sweden’s increased from 3.77 to 5.25. In China, it
climbed from 2.03 to 3.33. Median Chinese net financial
wealth increased substantially, but average wealth grew
faster. China thus illustrates a central tension of rapid
wealth creation: prosperity can spread while inequality
rises at the same time.
More broadly, the limited movement in both distribution

measures across most countries suggests that wealth
inequality is deeply entrenched. Two decades of
economic growth, crises and political attention have
barely altered the underlying distribution.

China was not alone. The top-decile share increased by
10.5pps in Russia and by 6.8pps in India, where rising
wealth concentration is a “side effect” of rapid growth.
Concentration also rose in several advanced economies.

In the US, the richest 10% increased their share by 4.0pps
to 68.2%, one of the highest levels among advanced
markets and with no improvement in sight. Sweden and
Switzerland also recorded increases of more than 4pps.
France saw a smaller rise of 3.1pps, although its top-
decile share of 54.8% remained below Germany’s 59.5%.
Rising concentration is therefore not confined to emerging
economies, nor does rapid development inevitably produce
it.

Appendix A: Methodological comments
General assumptions
The Allianz Global Wealth Report analyses gross financial assets held by households, i.e. cash and bank deposits,
receivables from insurance companies and pension institutions, securities (shares, bonds and investment funds) and other
receivables, as well as household liabilities. Net financial assets are defined as gross financial assets minus liabilities.
Real estate is excluded from this measure and analyzed separately where data are available. The report is based

on data from 57 countries. This group of countries covers 91% of global GDP and 72% of the global population. In 43
countries, we had access to statistics from the macroeconomic financial accounts. In the other countries, we were able to
estimate the volume of total financial assets based on information from household surveys, bank statistics, statistics on
assets held in equities and bonds and technical reserves.
To eliminate exchange-rate distortions over time, financial assets and liabilities denominated in national currencies are

converted into euros using exchange rates at the end of 2025. Unless explicitly described as real, these values are not
adjusted for inflation. The closing date for data to be included in the report is 31 July.
Statistical distinctions
The process associated with the introduction of the European System of Accounts 2010 (ESA 2010) in September 2014
involved updating and harmonizing the guidelines governing the preparation of many macroeconomic statistics. The

new requirements also apply to the macroeconomic financial accounts. One change relates to private households: under
the ESVG 2010 regulations, the two sectors „Private households“ and „Non-profit institutions serving households“ are
now reported separately. This also has implications for the Allianz Global Wealth Report, which takes data from the
macroeconomic financial accounts as a basis where available. For many countries, however – particularly those outside

of the EU – there is no separate data available for these sectors in general. So in order to ensure global comparability,
this publication analyzes both sectors together under the heading „private households“.
Determination of wealth bands for global wealth classes
Lower wealth threshold: There is a close link between financial assets and the incomes of private households. According
to Davies et al. (2009) , private individuals with below-average income tend to have no assets at all, or only very few. It is

only when individuals move into middle and higher income groups that they start to accumulate substantial assets.
We have applied this link to our analysis. Countries in the upper-middle income bracket (based on the World Bank‘s
country classification system) therefore form the group in which the average household assets first reach a meaningful
level. This value marks the lower threshold for the global middle wealth class.
In terms of income, households with incomes that correspond to between 75% and 150% of average net income are

generally considered to constitute the middle class. According to Davies et al., households with income corresponding
to 75% of the average income have assets that correspond to 30% of the average assets. As far as the upper threshold
is concerned, 150% of average income corresponds to 180% of average assets. Consequently, we have set the threshold
values for the wealth middle class at 30% and 180% of average net financial assets per capital. In 2025, average net

financial assets per capita in the countries covered stood at approximately EUR36,690. Applying these percentages gives
the rounded thresholds used in the workbook: EUR11,000 and EUR66,000. Individuals with net financial assets between
these thresholds belong to the global middle-wealth class. Those below the lower threshold belong to the low-wealth
class, while those above the upper threshold belong to the high-wealth class.

14  Davies, James B. et al. (2009), The level and distribution of global household wealth, NBER working paper 15508.

Economy 
Business Checkpoint: Looking into the logistics 
15 October 2026

Key takeaways 
• Small and mid-sized businesses entered Q3 in relatively solid financial condition, with profitability growth up across both 
groups, according to Bank of America business account data. Nearly half of estimated tariff payments had been refunded by 
September, though the share of these refunds relative to revenue has peaked. 
• In Bank of America business payments data, logistics spending growth per client was up four times as much for small than mid-
sized firms. Additionally, distribution and inventory transactions growth per client has outpaced spending since the spring of 
2025. 
• Growing transportation bottlenecks are re-emerging ahead of the holiday season. Trucking capacity has tightened as driver 
availability remains constrained, according to BofA Global Research. This has pushed freight rates higher and driven strong 
transportation sector payroll growth, particularly among mid-sized businesses, according to Bank of America payments data.

Business Checkpoint is a new, regular publication from Bank of America Institute. It provides a real-time view of the financial health, opportunities 
and challenges facing small and mid-sized businesses across the US economy, leveraging the depth and breadth of Bank of America's proprietary 
data. By analyzing activity among both small and mid-sized firms, Business Checkpoint offers timely insights into business profitability, hiring, 
investment and spending trends. Such data is not intended to be reflective or indicative of, and should not be relied upon as, the results of operations, 
financial condition or performance of Bank of America. 
Businesses end Q3 in fair shape 
While profitability growth remained positive for both small and mid-sized firms in the third quarter, small firms outpaced their 
mid-sized counterparts for the second consecutive quarter (Exhibit 2). Still, there’s been a notable reversal in the profitability of 
mid-sized businesses, growing this quarter after declining slightly for the past two.  
The continued rollout of tariff refunds is serving as a tailwind to some firms' cash positions. That is particularly true for small 
firms. According to Bank of America account data, by September approximately 47% of estimated International Emergency 
Economic Powers Act-related tariff payments had been refunded to companies with less than $50 million in annual revenue.

According to Bank of America business payments data, the percentage point difference in tariff refunds as a share of revenue 
for firms with less than $50 million in annual revenue spiked in June but dropped to nearly zero in September. At the same time, 
the percentage point difference in tariff payments as a share of expenses began rising again relative to the 2023 average 
(Exhibit 1). This suggests that while tariffs continue to affect companies, the refund program has provided temporary relief for 
some businesses.  
Wholesale trade along with transportation and warehousing posted solid profitability growth across business size 
However, the benefits appear uneven. Such refunds have boosted cash flow, but they have not fully insulated firms from broader 
cost pressures.  
Looking across industries, mid-sized construction and wholesale firms had the strongest profitability growth in September 
(Exhibit 3). Notably, transportation and warehousing, as well as wholesale trade were among the few sectors showing positive 
profitability growth for both small and mid-sized firms last month. 
The persistence of logistics, freight and inventory-related cost pressures may help explain why profitability gains have remained 
relatively concentrated in transportation and wholesale trade. However, these sectors also have a higher exposure to rising gas 
prices within their margins (read more on this in the August Small Business Checkpoint). 
Exhibit 3: For both small and mid-sized businesses, profitability growth was positive in transportation and warehousing as well as wholesale trade 
in September 
Profitability growth by sector and business size in September (YoY%, six-month moving average)

Source: Bank of America internal data  
BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The cost of maintaining supply chains is rising 
In fact, the prices paid component of the September services reading from the Institute for Supply Management indicates still-
building price pressure for firms (Exhibit 4). This marked the highest reading since July 2022.   
Using Bank of America internal data, we identified payments to logistics and freight companies to assess a key part of supply 
chain costs. In June, logistics and freight payments’ growth per client from small businesses grew nearly 30%, though has 
moderated to 12.7% YoY in September (Exhibit 5). Meanwhile, mid-sized firms’ payments growth in this category was up just 
3.2% YoY in September. 
The divergence suggests logistics costs may be disproportionately affecting smaller firms, who are typically less capable of 
absorbing rising costs than larger firms. Larger companies also often benefit from dedicated logistics contracts and greater 
purchasing power, while smaller ones may be more exposed to spot-market transportation costs and fluctuations in freight 
pricing.

Evidence of more moving parts in companies’ supply chains 
Yet there is increasing evidence that tariffs and geopolitics are adding complexity to small and mid-sized companies’ supply 
chains. 
In Bank of America payments data, average distribution and inventory transaction growth per business client has increased 
steadily since the spring of 2025 despite spending growth in this category falling in September (Exhibit 6). Rising transaction 
growth alongside softer spending growth suggests businesses may be engaging with more suppliers, shipments or inventory 
movements even as overall procurement outlays moderates.  
Furthermore, in May 2026, the Global Supply Chain Pressure Index reached its highest reading since the summer of 2022 and has 
continued to remain elevated into October (Exhibit 7). This further emphasizes that transportation bottlenecks, delays and 
procurement challenges are becoming more common globally. While current readings remain below the extreme levels observed 
during the pandemic, the recent move higher indicates supply chain normalization may be reversing.

The trials of trucking  
As of October 1, BofA Global Research's proprietary bi-weekly Truckload Demand Indicator (see Methodology) remained below 
its historical growth signal (Exhibit 9) and the Truck Capacity Indicator, which reflects shippers' views of available capacity, 
tightened significantly, falling from 39.2 to 28.4 (Exhibit 8). This suggests shippers are seeing fewer trucks available relative to 
demand than in the previous survey. 
According to BofA Global Research, the current trucking upcycle is being driven by constrained supply rather than a significant 
improvement in freight demand. While any positive movement in demand is an important indicator as the industry moves into 
peak shipping season between late September and early December, tighter capacity appears to be the dominant force 
supporting freight rates. 
Exhibit 8: Shippers’ view of available capacity has fallen 
significantly as of October 1, 2026

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE

The reduction in capacity reflects several factors, including stricter commercial driver's license (CDL) and training requirements, 
increased compliance and enforcement measures, and other regulatory changes that have reduced the pool of qualified drivers. 
This has also likely driven some wage inflation within the industry. According to Bank of America payments data, transportation 
and warehousing payroll growth per client has surged since the start of the year, especially for mid-sized businesses (Exhibit 10).  
Having to pay higher wages suggests labor shortages remain a challenge in the sector. Firms may increasingly need to offer 
higher compensation to attract and retain workers as trucking capacity tightens. In turn, such capacity puts upward pressure on 
freight rates, particularly in the spot market where small businesses may be more impacted.

Logistics payments tend to increase in the second half of the year 
September marks a key run-up to the holiday season (read more on signs of holiday spending in the October Consumer 
Checkpoint), with spending on ocean shipping, containers, and other transportation services steadily accelerating ahead of the 
peak shopping period.  
In fact, over the past two years, the month of November has seen ocean shipping/container spending volumes well above their 
2023-2025 monthly average (Exhibit 11). The seasonal pattern likely reflects inventory restocking and holiday-related shipping 
activity. Ocean shipping volumes typically accelerate during the fall months as businesses prepare for year-end demand. 
Exhibit 11: Ocean shipping/container spending volumes were well above their 2023-2025 monthly average in November 
Logistics and freight average payments by category (monthly, indexed, 100 = monthly 2023-2025 average)

BANK OF AMERICA INSTITUTE 
Distributors and inventory payments’ growth fell ahead of holiday season 
Changes in logistics and freight spending often provide an early signal of inventory trends, as distributors account for a large 
share of supply chain-related spending (Exhibit 12). 
The sharp moderation in distribution and inventory spending growth per client for both small and mid-sized businesses indicates 
that much of the large-scale inventory accumulation seen earlier in 2025 has already occurred (Exhibit 13). In other words, 
businesses appear to be replenishing inventories for seasonal demand rather than significantly expanding stockpiles as they did 
earlier in the year. 
Overall, businesses seem to be entering the holiday season in relatively stable financial condition, supported by tariff refunds 
and positive profitability growth. However, rising logistics costs, tighter trucking capacity and elevated supply chain pressures 
suggest operating conditions are becoming more challenging.

Methodology 
Selected Bank of America transaction data is used to inform the macroeconomic views expressed in this report and should be considered in the 
context of other economic indicators and publicly available information. In certain instances, the data may provide directional and/or predictive 
value. The data used is not comprehensive; it is based on aggregated and anonymized selections of Bank of America data and may reflect a 
degree of selection bias and limitations on the data available. 
Any Business payments data represents aggregate spend from Small Business and/or Business Banking clients with a deposit account or a 
Small Business credit card. Payroll payments data include channels such as ACH (automated clearing house), bill pay, checks and wire. Bank of 
America per Business client data represents activity spending from active Business clients with a deposit account or a Small Business credit 
card and at least one transaction in each month. Small businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America and are 
generally defined as under $5mm in annual sales revenue. Mid-sized businesses in this report include business clients within Bank of America 
and are generally defined as $5mm-$50mm in annual sales revenue. 
Unless otherwise stated, data is not adjusted for seasonality, processing days or portfolio changes, and may be subject to periodic revisions.  
Revenue tiers are determined by the combination of following factors: 1) stated revenue on small businesses credit applications, 2) actual 
account inflow into Bank of America Deposit Accounts, and 3) third party revenue estimation.  
The alternative hiring indicator consists of payments from Bank of America business clients to business-focused hiring firms which include both 
direct deposits through Automated Clearing House (ACH) and payments via credit and debit cards. 
Supply chain-related spending is comprised of payments to logistics, freight, distribution, inventory, delivery service, and trucking companies. 
These are identified from merchant names and MCC codes. 
BofA Global Research’s trucking indicators are a bi-weekly diffusion-style survey of shipper demand expectations over the next 0–3 months, 
built from responses from surveyed shippers. 
Data regarding merchants who receive payments are identified and classified by the Merchant Categorization Code (MCC) defined by financial 
services companies. The data are mapped using proprietary methods from the MCCs to the North American Industry Classification System 
(NAICS), which is also used by the Census Bureau, in order to classify spending data by subsector. Spending data may also be classified by other 
proprietary methods not using MCCs.  
Additional information about the methodology used to aggregate the data is available upon request.

2 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
The Five Shocks of 2026—And the Extraordinary Resilience of the U.S. Economy
Joseph Quinlan, Managing Director and Head of Market Strategy
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
This is the year the consensus got mugged.
Rewind to the start of the year. The market consensus was that the U.S. economy was
set to slow. The Fed was set to cut interest rates. Corporate earnings were set to grow—

but not explode. Energy prices would remain tame. And the odds looked good that the
Republicans would control the House, the Senate, or both, following the midterm election.
Nine months on, the landscape looks starkly different. Defined by five shocks—energy,
interest rates, earnings, growth, and politics—2026 looks nothing like the year investors
thought they were buying on January 1.
Shock 1: Diesel Matters. While global oil prices have moved higher since the start of

the U.S./Iran war on February 28, oil prices have yet to surpass their previous record high
because the world has released some 400 million barrels from strategic reserves this year,
China has curbed its demand for oil imports, and the U.S. has goosed its energy exports to
record highs.
But here is the problem: While gasoline powers the American consumer, diesel powers the
American economy—and diesel prices are now sitting at record highs. Diesel supplies have

been hammered by wars in the Middle East and Ukraine that have sharply reduced refining
capacity. According to the International Energy Agency, by August, exports of diesel from
refiners in the Middle East had fallen by a quarter from prewar levels. Meanwhile, Ukraine
has hit 24 of Russia’s 34 largest refineries, reducing supplies from one of the world’s
largest diesel exporters. The upshot: U.S. diesel prices have soared from $3.55 a gallon
at the start of the year to roughly $6.52 a gallon. While crude oil trades for around $100

a barrel, the retail equivalent of a barrel of diesel now costs more than $260. The diesel
shock of 2026 was the last thing investors saw coming—and remains a key impediment to
structurally lower inflation.
Shock 2: From Interest Rate Cuts to Rate Hikes. Perhaps no other consensus view
entering 2026 has been turned more dramatically on its head than expectations for the
Fed. Heading into 2026, markets were pricing in rate cuts on expectations of a cooling

labor market, moderating inflation, and slower economic growth. The Fed easing cycle was
expected to continue in 2026. In January 2026, futures showed a roughly 50% chance of a
cut by March.
Expectations, however, flipped in late February with the start of the U.S./Iran war. The
Middle East energy shock reignited inflationary pressures, which, combined with stronger-
than-expected U.S. economic growth and a tightening labor market, have demolished the

odds of easier money in 2026. Short- and long-term Treasury yields rose sharply last week
in the wake of the S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index (PMI)
which pointed to the strongest business expansion in more than five years and the fastest
employment growth in over four years, while also reinforcing expectations that the Fed
will maintain a restrictive policy stance for longer. Markets were expecting two to three
quarter-point rate cuts in 2026; instead, the Fed has already raised rates once this year and

is expected to keep raising rates over the near term.
Shock 3: The Earnings Boom No One Saw Coming. Not all the shocks of 2026 are
negative. Indeed, stronger-than-expected corporate earnings have been among the most
positive factors of this year. Corporate America entered the year facing questions about
tariffs, AI-related capital expenditure spending, the health of the U.S. consumer, operating
leverage and the U.S. dollar outlook. At the start of the year, Wall Street was expecting

roughly $290 of S&P 500 earnings per share for 2026, per Bloomberg estimates. But by
September 21, the estimate had risen by $50, to $340 per share.
Investment Implications
While some of these shocks may
contribute to periods of market
volatility, the combination of strong
earnings growth, robust domestic
demand, and ongoing capital
investment suggests that the
fundamental backdrop for Equities
remains constructive.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Earnings have been upgraded and underpinned by several factors, including robust final 
domestic demand in the U.S., a pickup in global growth, AI-related capital spending, 
productivity gains, and enhanced operating leverage. All these variables have helped deliver 
an earnings “shock” to Wall Street. And as an aside, yes, headline profit growth has been 
inflated by strong technology earnings and one-off investment gains from Amazon and 
Alphabet. That said, the S&P 500’s earnings story remains extraordinary. According to 
FactSet, the 493 companies excluding the Magnificent 7 1 
posted 31.8% earnings growth in 
Q2, the strongest level since late 2021.

Shock 4: An Economy with more Horsepower than the Headlines Suggest. That 
corporate profits have exceeded expectations this ye
ar is due in no small part to an economy 
that continues to surprise to the upside. The Fed was supposed to cut rates this year to 
keep the economy humming and prevent it from losing steam. But rather than limp through 
2026, the economy has leaped ahead. Yes, real gross domestic product (GDP) growth of 
2.1% in Q1 and 1.5% in Q2 are hardly blockbuster annualized rates of growth. However, the 
figures belie the underlying strength of the U.S. economy, with real final domestic demand

(which strips out trade) running at a 4.2% annual rate in Q2. According to the latest reading 
of the Atlanta Fed GDP tracker, Q3 real GDP grow
th is running at a red-hot rate of 5.1%. 
Slowdown? What slowdown?
Resilient consumer spending, robust capital investment, record leve
ls of new business 
formation, and strong foreign direct investment inflows have combined to stoke growth 
in the $32 trillion behemoth called the U.S. economy—the most resilient and dynamic 
economy in the world.
Shock 5: The Unexpected Washington Reset. The last shock is still in the making, but 
given current trends, the odds are rising that the Democrats could control both the House 
and Senate follow
ing the midterm elections. To this point, in January, and per Kalshi, the 
Democrats had a 71% shot at the House and an 18% chance of taking the Senate. Fast-
forward to today, and the odds are a 90% chance that the Democrats take the House and a 
60% chance that they take the Senate.
How all of this plays out remains to be seen—there are some 36 days before the November 
vote, and anything can happen. The market base case remains that the Democrats take the 
House, and the Senate is a toss-up—or too close to call. But with the Democrats expected to 
control at least one chamber of Congress, inv
estors can expect more volatility and uncertainty 
about negotiations ov
er the budget, debt ceiling, defense spending and AI regulation. Another 
political wildcard to watch: the changing dynamics within the Democratic Party.
The Biggest Shock of All for 2026? Pe
rhaps it’s not $6 diesel, rising interest rates, 
solid growth, booming profits or shifting politics. Rather, it’s the ability of the U.S. 
economy to absorb the unexpected, not buckle under stress, and continue generating  wealth 
for investors. To wit, the S&P 500 is on pace for its fourth
 consecutive year of double-digit 
returns.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 28, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
2026 Has, So Far, Defied the Traditional Midterm Year Playbook
Jordy Fuentes, Assistant Vice President and Investment Strategist
Current Landscape. To no surprise, market volatility persisted in 2026. Performance initially
tracked historical midterm election patterns before diverging meaningfully as the year
progressed, though there are still three months to go. Several items remain at hand as U.S.

voters head to the polls in November, including geopolitical and foreign policy uncertainty,
energy driven inflation pressures, affordability concerns, U.S. fiscal trajectory, and rapid
advancements in AI. Regardless of the eventual political outcome, history suggests that
markets and the economy can perform well across different configurations of government.
In our view, markets continue to be anchored by strong fundamentals and encouraging
seasonal trends.

To Be Addressed. U.S. domestic and foreign policy remain in focus as the global reorder,
tit-for-tat tariffs, and geopolitical conflicts spanning the Middle East to Eastern Europe have
added layers of uncertainty to the U.S. economic outlook.
At the same time, persistent geopolitical flare-ups have pushed oil prices materially
higher, adding to inflation pressures and keeping them above the Fed’s 2% target.
Headline Consumer Price Index (CPI) held above 3% year-over-year (YoY) in August, while

its core measure, which excludes food and energy, held at 2.4% YoY. The latest Personal
Consumption Expenditure (PCE), the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation, also remained
above 3% YoY for both its headline and core figures. 2
The U.S. fiscal trajectory is another area of focus. The U.S. budget deficit stands at 5.4% of
GDP , just above this century’s average of 4.6%. Meanwhile, U.S. public sector debt surpassed
the $40 trillion mark for the first time in August, oscillating around 100% of GDP .3 While these

trends warrant close monitoring, we view them as a slow-moving structural headwind rather
than an immediate concern, particularly within a highly innovative and diverse U.S. economy.
AI has emerged as a policy consideration, with debates increasingly centered on both
regulatory safeguards and the economic implications of the expanding infrastructure buildout.
For voters, those concerns extend to affordability, as the significant energy requirements of

new data centers raise questions about potential pressure on electricity costs.
Strap In for The Ride. Leading up to the midterm election, markets tend to experience
greater swings as investors navigate uncertainty surrounding the policy path for the
remainder of the presidential cycle (Exhibit 2A). Since 1930, the S&P 500 has averaged an
intrayear drawdown of 20% in midterm years, with the latest election in 2022 experiencing
a 25% intrayear pullback. This year, however, the S&P 500 largest peak-to-trough drawdown

has been 9%, considerably shallower than its long-term average (Exhibit 2B). Yet while
volatility may rise as election day approaches, 2026 has increasingly defied the traditional
midterm year playbook.
Why 2026 is Different. Despite entering a challenging stretch of the presidential cycle,
U.S. Equities have materially outpaced the typical year two pattern (Exhibit 2C). The S&P
500 experienced its best Q2 in a midterm year since 1938, surging 14.9% compared to an

average decline of 1.6%. From a monthly perspective, September stands out as the weakest
month, though month-to-date performance points to an unusual 1.0% rise. That resilience
follows a 2.6% gain in August, another month in which performance meaningfully exceeded
the historical decline of 0.5% (Exhibit 2D).
Through September, U.S. Equity resilience has also extended beyond headline returns with
both the frequency and magnitude of market pullbacks remaining relatively subdued. Market

corrections of 10% or more have occurred in just 29% of midterm election years since 1930.4
Against this backdrop, the market’s last correction came in spring 2025, while this year has

seen only one 5%+ pullback compared to the average three per year, underscoring relatively
resilient market conditions.5 Year-to-date, the S&P 500 has increased 13%, well above the
average annual return of 3% according to Bloomberg through September 25, and on pace
to mark the strongest midterm election year since 2006. Taken together, 2026 has thus far
defied the traditionally weak midterm year seasonal pattern, reinforcing that every election

cycle is unique and historical trends may be a useful guide to navigate market volatility.
What’s Ahead? Forward performance is increasingly constructive for Equities. In the
post-war period, the S&P 500 rose 100% of the time in the six and 12 months following
elections, averaging 13% and 14%, respectively. 6 Even narrowing the time frame to three
months, markets ended higher 90% of the time. As investors push through the finish line,
the last three months of the year tend to be the strongest with October standing out,

ending higher 67% of the time. On average, Q4 has gained 5.6% since 1930, far surpassing
the preceding three quarters of election years. 7 Greater clarity about the U.S. fiscal policy
path serves as a potential tailwind for investors following the election.
To Take Home. Volatility tends to rise during midterm years, but history suggests that
reacting to short-term political uncertainty has rarely rewarded long-term investors. Over
longer horizons, the data suggests Equity returns and economic strength are shaped less

by election outcomes than by corporate profits, valuations, labor market conditions and
capital investments.

THE TICKER TAPE
What is the bond market telling us?
Christopher Hyzy, Chief Investment Office
We believe the bond market is telling us that the Fed is on track to meeting their dual
mandate of price stability and full employment. More importantly, when examining the
full yield curve the 2-year Treasury yield is currently around 4.88%, which typically is
the most sensitive to the fed funds rate and tends to price in future potential policy

adjustments. At the same time, the 10-year Treasury yield is currently about 5.11%. This
leads us to believe that the market expects the Fed to engineer a couple of more hikes
while the back end of the curve is indicating that economic growth is still healthy.
Taken together, it appears the full curve, which has clearly flattened, believes the Fed is
doing its job, while supply shocks, for obvious reasons, are not leading to higher long-run
inflation. The backup in long-dated yields is growth, not necessarily inflation as indicated

by rising “real” yields. Therefore, we view the drift higher in yields as a more normal sign
than what many have originally suggested. We continue to emphasize higher economic
growth for longer, rising productivity in the medium term, supply shortages to remain in
key areas within the AI space, and shocks in the energy equation. We expect the energy
shocks to ultimately fade away over time, but we do not believe that growth fades
anytime soon. In our view, a more normal bond market pricing in Fed moves at the short

end, and growth at the long end helps financial conditions and economic growth operate
in a more durable manner.
In summary, the front-end of the curve expects 3 to 4 more hikes in the next 18 months
or so. This can be debated obviously based on future data especially short-term energy
supply shocks. Long-end yields are drifting higher based on five main factors below, in
our view.
1. Strong economic growth (both
services and manufacturing)
2. Hawkish Fed

3. Debt and deficit concerns
4. Lower demand for long duration
sovereign debt in general
5. Energy supply shocks
The largest weight being strong economic activity which kick starts portfolio
repositioning particularly as the Fed hikes rates. The repositioning stops once demand
rises again when yields reach attractive levels for the more patient longer term
investment community. The supply shocks ultimately end and debt and deficit worries will
likely remain, but they still take a backseat for now. Longer duration government debt is

becoming more attractive each week even in the face of the concerns above. Be patient

Index Definitions
Securities indexes assume reinvestment of all distributions and interest payments. Indexes are unmanaged and do not take into account fees or expenses. It is not possible to
invest directly in an index. Indexes are all based in U.S. dollars.
S&P 500 Index is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States.

S&P 500 Index Price Return is a stock market index tracking the stock performance of 500 leading companies listed on stock exchanges in the United States. A price return index is a stock
market index that tracks only the price changes and capital gains or losses of its component securities, without including dividends or cash distributions.
S&P Global Flash U.S. Composite Purchasing Managers’ Index is an early monthly economic indicator that estimates overall private-sector business activity by combining manufacturing

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.

investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

EL NIÑO 2026–2027: MULTIPLE PRESSURES ON PRICES AND SUPPLIES
Hélène Drouot
The 2026–2027 El Niño climate event is expected to be one of the most intense in the last fifty years, with sea-surface-temperature
anomalies potentially exceeding +3°C in the equatorial Pacific. According to the World Meteorological Organisation (WMO), El Niño
is expected to persist until February 2027, with asymmetrical effects, as South-East Asia and northern Latin America will experience

droughts, while southern Brazil and Argentina could see heavy rainfall. Above all, El Niño is compounding the effects of global warming
and exacerbating existing extreme-weather events (droughts, floods and heatwaves). The economic effects of this climate shock mainly
play out through three avenues: agricultural commodity prices, energy and logistics (see heatmap).
Agricultural commodity prices: volatility is causing tensions, with inflationary effects

In India and South-East Asia, droughts will affect harvests, pushing up prices (particularly for coffee, cocoa, rice and palm oil). Conversely, in
southern Brazil and Argentina, heavy rainfall will boost soya and maize production, putting downward pressure on prices. According to the IMF,
El Niño causes global food prices to rise by between 5% (in a typical episode) and 8% (very high intensity) year-on-year, with the full impact being
felt 6 to 12 months after the climatic peak.

Therefore, for a given country, the weighting of food in its inflation index (CPI) plays a decisive role. As a result, all other things being equal, an 8%
rise in prices adds around 1 pp to inflation when food accounts for 13% of the CPI basket (average for countries shown in green on the heatmap),
1.8 pp when this share reaches 22% (yellow), 2.8 pp at 35% (orange) and nearly 4 pp above 49% (red).
The risks are skewed to the upside, as the 8% estimate does not take into account additional losses linked to droughts, floods and other extre -

me events, while policy responses to shocks (stockpiling by importers and increased export restrictions) may amplify and prolong price pres -
sures. The rice market between 2023 and 2024 illustrates this well, as the combination of rice-export restrictions imposed by India in July 2023
(in order to protect its domestic market) and of the drought in South-East Asia contributed to a 30% average increase in the price of rice over the
course of 2023 and kept global prices high for several months after the end of the El Niño episode.

Energy production sector: heavy reliance on hydroelectricity leaves the sector vulnerable to a second supply shock
Droughts will reduce hydroelectric power generation in Latin America and South-East Asia. As a result, some countries will have to turn to other,
more expensive solutions, such as fossil fuels, against a backdrop of already high oil and gas prices. This shift will increase global demand for
oil and put pressure on prices which, according to the IMF, could persist for up to four quarters after the initial El Niño shock. For example,

in Colombia, where hydropower accounts for 55% of electricity generation, a 30% drop in output (already seen in 2023–2024) could increase the
cost of electricity generation by 15 to 20% (assuming that the cost of thermal power generation is twice as high as hydropower). In Ecuador (where
hydropower accounts for 70% of electricity generation) and Vietnam (30%), electricity shortages are likely to disrupt local industries.
SOURCE: IMF, WORLD BANK, EMBER, BNP PARIBAS CALCULATIONS

Vulnerabilities differ, depending on a country’s dependence on food, energy and logistics

Reading note: Scores are standardised on a scale of 0 to 10 for ease of comparison between countries and supply channels.
- Food vulnerability: based on the weight of food in the CPI.
- Energy vulnerability: based on the share of hydropower in electricity generation.
- Logistics vulnerability: the ‘Trade and transport-related infrastructure’ component of the World Bank’s Logistics Perfor-

mance Index, which measures the quality of the infrastructure required for transport and international trade.

Chart of the Week / 30 September 2026 economic-research.bnpparibas.com
Logistics: transport infrastructure is another point of vulnerability
The final channel relates to disruptions to freight transport against a backdrop of already weakened value chains. The variable used in our
heatmap is a proxy for economies’ abilities to absorb a shock (particularly a climate-related one) affecting their transport infrastructure (for

example, by maintaining traffic flow, diverting or reorganising transport flows). The heatmap highlights the particularly high vulnerability
of Latin America. Its capacity to absorb a logistics shock is relatively limited, and there is significant exposure at a strategic chokepoint, the
Panama Canal, through which approximately 5% of global trade passes. Low rainfall in the canal basin has already led to the number of
daily transits being reduced in September 2026 (as in 2023–2024); further restrictions are expected in October (to 29 transits per day, down

from 36 in August), which will increase transport times and costs. Europe is also being affected, with low water levels on the Rhine and the
Danube already disrupting river transport and electricity generation.
Latin America and South-East Asia are hit hardest
Therefore, the economies most at risk are not necessarily those that will suffer the greatest climate shock, but those where multiple trans -
mission channels converge, including a high weighting of food prices in inflation, reliance on hydroelectricity, fragile transport infrastructure

and limited fiscal room for manoeuvre. Latin America and South-East Asia are home to some of the most vulnerable countries, particularly
due to their dependence on food prices and hydroelectricity. Colombia and Ecuador are noteworthy for their high exposure to energy risk,
while Ghana, India and the Philippines are some of the economies most vulnerable to a food shock. Latin America faces a combination of
vulnerabilities, such as high exposure to food and energy price shocks in some countries, which are compounded by logistics risks, as currently

illustrated by the restrictions on traffic through the Panama Canal.
helene.drouot@bnpparibas.com

Document 310: FX Daily_ Inflation vs. AI overbuild story dominates

Source PDF: all report pdf/FX Daily_ Inflation vs. AI overbuild story dominates.pdf
Report source: Unknown
Date: 未知日期
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Financial markets have calmed a little after Coreweave stock
bounced on Thursday. But the debate between intensifying
price pressures needing tighter monetary policy and a tech
correction requiring a softer policy outlook continues. Expect
more consolidation in FX markets today and more interest in
local stories, such as Indonesia's equity travails

Chris Turner, Frantisek Taborsky and Francesco Pesole
FX markets remain caught between intensifying inﬂation and a re-pricing of AI
risk
USD: What does the ﬂatter yield curve
mean?
The sell-oﬀ on Coreweave assets saw a modest reversal yesterday
after Nvidia's CEO, Jensen Huang, said that Coreweave's ﬁnancial
strength was improving and its cost of capital was declining.
Perhaps those comments should not be surprising from a major
shareholder in Coreweave; let's see how long this bounce in
Coreweave assets lasts. The big takeaway this week has been the
recovery of the long-end of the US Treasury market, even as

inﬂation data continues to come in hot. Here it seems that
investors are starting to ﬁnd value in long-dated Treasuries and are
viewing those as a middling hedge against a correction in the AI
trade. Were the tech sell-oﬀ to gain momentum, that Treasury
buying would move to the shorter end of the curve.
While fears of an AI correction may be growing, inﬂation data
continues to come in hot. Yesterday it was US September PPI data
blowing away consensus. And today, import prices should come in

near 8% year-on-year, the highest since summer 2022. Inﬂation
data is keeping short-dated interest rates supported and has
delivered a near 20bp ﬂattening in the 2-10 year Treasury curve
this week.
What does that mean for FX? Typically, an environment where the
Fed is applying the brakes while growth prospects are being revised
down is good for the dollar against activity and emerging market
currencies. It is only when short end rates break lower that the

dollar is ready to drop. And we are not there yet.
Barring another big leg down in AI stocks and risk assets in general,
expect the dollar to consolidate near recent highs. We would
continue to expect some defensive outperformance of the Swiss
franc in this environment. DXY will probably struggle to get above
the 102.40/50 area now, while support is 101.75/80.
Chris Turner
EUR: Inﬂation to be conﬁrmed at 3.8%
today
Today's eurozone data should see September CPI conﬁrmed at

3.8% – well above the European Central Bank's 2% target. Markets
are still reeling from the hawkish set of inﬂation forecasts delivered
at the ECB September press conference, a key driver of the near
75bp of further tightening expected from the ECB. Our team looks
for just one more hike, however, and developments in French bond
markets will have a big say in whether the ECB needs to tighten
much more from there.

EUR/USD has received a lift from this week's unease in US AI stocks
and may be entering a decent consolidative phase now. This could

Chris Turner
CEE: Wage embers and price sparks
The CEE week ends with mostly second-tier data. Hungary releases
August wage ﬁgures this morning, one of the central bank’s

remaining domestic obstacles to achieving stable inﬂation. We
forecast wage growth of 7.1%, still well above CEE peers, while the
agreed minimum wage increase raises the risk of renewed wage
pressures next year.
Later, the Czech Republic publishes September producer prices.
Alongside agricultural prices, these suggest inﬂationary pressures
are broadening beyond fuel costs, posing an upside risk to the CPI
outlook, as discussed in our CEE report earlier this week. Turkey will

also release September inﬂation expectations, the ﬁnal relevant
data point before next week’s central bank meeting. We expect the
Central Bank of Turkey to resume its easing cycle, which was
paused after the US-Iran conﬂict began, by cutting the policy rate
from 37.0% to 36.0%.
CEE ﬁxed income saw a strong rally yesterday, tracking core
markets despite negative global sentiment; Hungary and Romania
have outperformed the rest of the region over the past two days.

We maintain the view that the koruna and zloty yield curves cannot
decouple from the global narrative, and that higher energy prices
will keep rate hikes priced in for longer. The Hungarian curve will
face a test next Monday or Tuesday with the release of the draft
budget for next year – an event drawing signiﬁcant market
attention, though expectations may be overly optimistic. FX, on the
other hand, remains more or less stable, and we continue to hold

our bearish views for the CEE region in general.
Frantisek Taborsky

IDR: MSCI downgrade warning sinks local
assets
In a statement that will have been a diﬃcult read for Indonesian
authorities, MSCI said overnight that it was launching a
consultation to reclassify MSCI Indonesia indexes from Emerging

Market to Standalone Market status. Merely dropping Indonesia to
Frontier Markets would not properly recognise the depth of the
market problems, says MSCI. These problems are opaque
shareholding structures and suspected coordinated trading
behaviour, according to MSCI.
Local equity indices fell 10-11% on the announcement and an
already weak rupiah gapped above the 18000 threshold. Expect
more FX intervention from Bank Indonesia to protect this year's

high at 18200 – but clearly pressure for a weaker rupiah is building.
Please see ING's Deepali Bhargava's latest comments on USD/IDR
here.
Indonesia's problems serve as a reminder that EMFX will
increasingly be driven by local stories over the coming quarters.
How the AI trade fares will dominate Korea and Taiwan, Brazilian
assets could be re-rated further on a Bolsonaro presidency, and
Hungary's euro ambitions will have a major say on local asset

markets. Please see our latest thinking on this topic in October's
edition of FX Talking.
Chris Turner

Document 344: Oil prices hold firm despite Persian Gulf supply growing

Source PDF: all report pdf/Oil prices hold firm despite Persian Gulf supply growing.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 5
Status: ok

### Page 1

Oil prices remain ﬁrm despite rising supply from the Persian
Gulf. We expect oil prices to trade lower into year-end. More
positive signs of a potential US-Iran deal would give us more
conﬁdence in this view. Meanwhile, European gas looks
vulnerable heading deeper into the heating season
Warren Patterson
Oil prices are holding ﬁrm despite Persian Gulf supply increasing
Persian Gulf oil ﬂows pick-up
There are few signs of de-escalation in the Middle East. If anything,

it appears as though the conﬂict is broadening, with the Houthis in
Yemen increasingly targeting Saudi Arabia. Despite little
improvement in the geopolitical environment, oil ﬂows from the
Persian Gulf have been trending higher with supply from the region
returning to 70-75% of pre-war levels, although there are some
estimates suggesting we could be back to as high as 80-90% of
pre-war ﬂows.
Cookie settings
At ING we respect your right to choose how your data is used.

please conﬁrm your preferences. You can change your cookie

Targeting Cookies
Conﬁrm selection Accept all

### Page 2

The fact that oil prices are well-supported despite the improvement
in ﬂows from the region appears to reﬂect concerns over how
sustainable this supply is, particularly when considering the
geopolitical backdrop. The market continues to price in a large risk
premium in the absence of a deal between the US and Iran. And to
see this risk premium erode, the market may need the comfort of a
deal. Failing that, time may be enough for oil prices to trend lower,

assuming supplies from the region continue to hold up. For now, we
are holding onto our 4Q26 Brent forecast of $80/bbl and FY27
forecast of $71/bbl. However, given the high level of uncertainty,
this is a low-conviction call, speciﬁcally for 4Q26.
As oil ﬂows from the Persian Gulf have picked up, another
bottleneck has become increasingly noticeable in the oil market.
There is signiﬁcant tightness in the tanker market, which has seen
tanker freight rates surge to record levels. Oil ﬂows from the Persian

Gulf have become increasingly ineﬃcient due to the challenging
environment, which has absorbed more of the global tanker ﬂeet.
And the only way this starts to normalise is if the way oil ﬂows
from the region also starts to normalise.
The other pain point for the oil market remains diesel, which will be
a concern as we head further into the Northern Hemisphere winter.
Disruptions from the Persian Gulf and Russia have tightened the
market, while the risk of export restrictions from the US has kept the

market nervous. The risk of US export restrictions appears to have
subsided, with Europe set to release diesel from strategic reserves.
Furthermore, expensive freight suggests that reﬁned product prices
are likely to remain elevated.
Natural gas vulnerable heading into heating
season
European natural gas prices have traded at their highest level since
December 2022, with supply concerns growing as the region heads
into the heating season. Unfortunately for the natural gas market,

we have not seen a similar recovery in LNG ﬂows from the Persian
Gulf as we have with oil. Therefore, LNG ﬂows from the region
remain down about 80% year-on-year. The market is increasingly

irrespective of a particular user's means, ﬁnancial situation or investment
accepting that Persian Gulf LNG could remain largely absent from
global markets for much of the Northern Hemisphere winter. This
means Asia and Europe will continue competing for spot cargoes
throughout the winter, keeping prices well supported and leaving
the market vulnerable to further spikes.
European gas storage remains a concern, at just under 75% full,

well below the ﬁve-year average of 89%. Low storage has seen the
German government instruct state-owned SEFE to buy and store an
additional 8TWh of natural gas by 15 December. The prospect of
essentially forced buying provides further support to the market.
With little sign of an imminent recovery in Persian Gulf LNG supply,
weather will be crucial this winter, and there is growing expectation
that Europe will experience a winter of milder temperatures due to

El Niño, which would provide some relief to the market. However, it
is not a strong relationship, and El Niño does not guarantee milder
temperatures. The North Asia winter will also be important for the
LNG market, as El Niño can also typically mean milder
temperatures in this region.
We now expect TTF to average EUR73/MWh over the 26/27 winter,
up from a previous forecast of EUR65/MWh. The market will likely be
very volatile over this period, given the lower starting point for gas

storage. Prices are then expected to sell oﬀ aggressively from 2Q27
as Persian Gulf supply normalises, and we see a further ramp-up in
US LNG export capacity.

Document 354: Rates Spark_ France still singled out

Source PDF: all report pdf/Rates Spark_ France still singled out.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-20
Pages: 3
Status: ok

### Page 1

Revived diplomatic eﬀorts around the Middle East lift market
sentiment, but French spreads still widened beyond 150bp.
Fiscal concerns and public unrest clearly weigh on French
government bonds (OATs), but more is at play
Benjamin Schroeder
Recent unrest is adding to concerns around President Macron and France's
ability to consolidate its ﬁnances
France remains singled out, but it's not pure
credit concerns
Risk sentiment was more upbeat after headlines suggesting a

revival of diplomatic eﬀorts to end the Middle East conﬂict. Energy
prices retreated from their recent highs, stocks advanced, and yields
edged lower. Still, markets have learnt to treat such headlines with
a healthy dose of scepticism.
Within the European government bond space, a tentative
stabilisation of spreads over Bunds mirrored the improved
sentiment. However, France continues to be singled out, with the
10y spread pushing past 150bp. We had cautioned that the public

Cookie settings
At ING we respect your right to choose how your data is used.
Here you can specify which type of cookies you consent to and
which you prefer to disable. Once you've made your selection,
please conﬁrm your preferences. You can change your cookie
preferences here at any time.
Read more about our Cookie policy
Essential
Performance Cookies
Targeting Cookies
Conﬁrm selection Accept all

### Page 2

unrest was adding to concerns around France's ability to
consolidate its ﬁnances. The unrest also adds very direct costs in
terms of lost GDP, as our economist warns.
Back during the days of the sovereign debt crisis, the French spread
versus Germany peaked at just below 200bp. But bear in mind, this
was when Italian spreads pushed past 500bp and markets were
contemplating the tail risk of a collapsing monetary union, i.e.,
redenomination risk.
To add some perspective, a 10y spread of close to 200bp in late

2011 meant that French bonds yielded more than twice as much as
German Bunds. The 10y Bund yield stood at around 1.9%.
Nowadays, French 10y bonds, at just below 5%, yield less than 1.5
times as much as their German counterpart. And that 5% mark in
outright yields appears to remain a hurdle for OATs for now.
This is not to say that spreads cannot push further, especially in the
absence of any good news and with a long way to go until the
presidential elections. But it is important to highlight that there are

other factors pushing spreads wider that do not directly reﬂect pure
credit concerns. Overall, rates volatility has increased and weighs on
spreads more generally. Especially when market liquidity is
subdued, this can exacerbate widening pressures across EGBs. The
French CDS spread, for that matter, while wider, is still 160bp away
from its 2011 peaks rather than a bit over 40bp when looking at the
bond spread.
Wednesday’s events and market view
Following the UK CPI release in the morning, it will be a quiet

day in terms of data. The main focus will be the public
appearances of BoE Governor Bailey and ECB President
Lagarde.
In the primary government bond markets, the US will auction
20y bonds (US$13bn). The European primary is quiet. Italy’s
two retail BTP Valore bonds are still open for subscription
through the week.
